We can recall here the sacred words, "Vēṇḍattakkatu aṟivōy nī vēṇḍa muzhuvatum taruvōy nī vēṇḍum parisu oṉṟu uṇḍu eṉṉil atuvum uṇdaṉ viruppam aṉṟē" (You know what is to be desired; you grant everything desired; if there is anything to be desired, that too is not your desire). Even when his intellect advised him thus, his mind again prompted him to go to Tillai, thinking, "I will go tomorrow, I will go tomorrow, I will go tomorrow." Many days passed in this manner. Every night, the desire to go to Tillai would arise intensely. At dawn, he would think of his situation and his birth-caste tradition, and regret deeply that it was not proper to go to Tillai. Then, as his love intensified again, he would think and desire, "I will go tomorrow, I will go tomorrow." Thus, Nandanar's mind experienced many states: the resolve to go to Tillai, then the suppression of that resolve because it was not in harmony with his caste, and then again the resolve. Because Nandanar thought "I will go tomorrow, I will go tomorrow" every day, remembering the Lord, he was named "Nalaippovar" or "TirunAlaippOvAr." It is the nature of great people not to delay good deeds. The reason for this is their realization of the transient nature of this birth. This birth is very short-lived, like a pulai flower. Pulai is a type of plant with flowers. Since its life is very short, the pulai flower is used as a simile for our birth. Nandanar also resolved to see Tillai to end his bondage of birth. In accordance with "seeing Tillai brings liberation," Nandanar set out from his ancient village of Adhanur. He set out from Adhanur, his ancient birthplace, surrounded by areca palms with abundant spathe flowers, and reached near Tillai, surrounded by fields where vaalai fish leap and jump. As soon as he saw Tillai, as soon as he saw its boundary, he bowed to that sacred boundary and rose. It is a truth established by sacred texts that bowing to the boundary of Tillai removes rebirth. At the boundary of Tillai where Nandanar arrived, there were public sacrificial halls and places where Vedas were studied. To Nandanar, who stood at the boundary of Tillai and bowed to it, the smoke of sacrifices and the places where Vedas were chanted became visible. Seeing them, he feared to go near those sacred places where Vedas were chanted, thinking of his low caste. Therefore, he did not go further but stood at the boundary of Tillai. What Nandanar saw at the boundary of Tillai were the sacrificial halls where many Vedic sages and Brahmins together performed sacrifices. Sekkizhar Peruman is going to describe in the following verses that these sacrificial halls are places where everyone comes together to perform sacrifices. Moreover, the three fires called "mutti," which the Brahmins maintain daily in their homes, are also present in the homes of the Brahmins within the city of Tillai. Nandanar, thinking of his birth-caste, stood without entering Tillai, adhering to a rule that one should not go near sacrificial halls and places where Vedas are studied, having been born in such a caste. The rules regarding which professions are appropriate for which lineage, and how one should behave when members of one lineage interact or stay with members of another, and who should be where, who should go where, and who is allowed to go where, all these rules are prescribed in Agamas and Smritis. In those days, people followed these rules and lived according to them. They performed dharma by adhering to these traditional rules. Nandanar was one who performed dharma by adhering to these traditional rules. Therefore, he also thought in this manner. Nandanar's situation is different. Similarly, Kannappa Nayanar's situation is different. He had immense love for the Lord with the topknot, forgetting caste, lineage, and other distinctions. Therefore, we must carefully understand that his situation was different. Thus, Nandanar, who stood at the boundary of Tillai, thought that if he crossed this boundary and these walls outside Tillai and went inside, there would be three thousand Tillai Brahmins who maintain sacrificial fires in their homes. They say there would be three thousand sacrificial fires in three thousand homes. If this is the case, it is not at all appropriate for him to go inside. It would be very difficult, Nandanar feared. Therefore, outside the wall of Tillai, his uncontainable great love grew more and more, his heart melted, he folded his hands, and he circumambulated the sacred boundary of Tillai many times, standing there, deeply moved. Nandanar, having set out from Adhanur and reached the boundary of Tillai, is thus at the southern gate of Tillai. Sekkizhar has composed, "Māḷigaigaḷ tōṟum kulavum vēdigaigaḷ oṉṟiya āgutigaḷ mūvāyiram uḷ eṉpār" (They say there are three thousand sacrificial fires united with altars in every mansion). By "vēdigaigaḷ oṉṟiya āgutigaḷ" (sacrificial fires united with altars), it is explained here that the three fires of the perpetual fire (nitya agni) maintained in each Brahmin's household, along with the altar and pit for those three fires, are meant. A "vēdigai" is a small platform placed in front of the sacrificial pit for placing sacred pots and other items for worship. The three thousand Tillai Brahmins perform the agnikāryam (fire ritual) in their respective three thousand mansions, each individually. Thus, by performing individual agnikāryam in three thousand homes, three thousand offerings are made there. "Kaṭṟāṅgu eri ōmpi kaliyai vārāmē seṭṟār" (They nurtured the fire as learned and prevented Kali from coming) is from Tirugnanasambandar's Koil Tiruppadigam. Nandanar, when he was staying in Adhanur, would think of going to Tillai. Then he would change his mind. He would say, "I will go tomorrow," because of his caste. Then he would change his mind again. After all this, he finally resolved and now came to the boundary of Tillai. Even when he came to the boundary of Tillai, he saw the sacrificial halls, the Brahmin homes, and the places where Vedas were studied, and he was mentally disturbed, thinking it would be very difficult to go past all these and enter. That thought prevented him from going further into Tillai. Because of this hindrance, his love for the Lord grew more and more, becoming an unquenchable great love. It grew, it intensified, his heart melted. Therefore, he circumambulated the wall many times and stood at the very boundary of Tillai, worshipping. Because he could not go inside, Nandanar's mind was agitated and melted, and this great love that arose grew immeasurably and overflowed, becoming indescribable. Unable to go inside Tillai, and unable to leave Tillai, Nandanar circled around and around like ants around a pot of honey. Just as a calf circles around a house when its mother cow is kept inside and it is left outside, so too Nandanar, unable to go and see the Lord who was dancing in the sacred hall inside Tillai, and unable to leave that place, circled around the city of Tillai like a calf searching for its mother. Thus, he circumambulated Tillai day and night. He was disheartened, thinking repeatedly about the nature of this lowly birth that prevented him from entering Tillai. He slept in sorrow, wondering how he could worship the sacred dance in the sacred hall of Lord Shiva, who has a dark throat. He slept, thinking that this lowly birth, which causes suffering, is an impediment to having a darshan of the Lord. He calls this birth a lowly birth because it is an impediment to having a darshan of the Lord. Thus, he slept, thinking that this lowly birth, which causes suffering, is an impediment to having a darshan of the Lord. In this state, Ambalavanar, who dances in the sacred hall, knowing Nandanar's condition, graced Nandanar. To alleviate all the sorrows of that pure devotee Nandanar, Tillai Kuttapperuman appeared in his dream. He appeared with a gentle smile and graced him. The Lord appeared in Nandanar's dream and told Nandanar, "So that this birth may cease, you shall plunge into the fire and come before Me with the Brahmins wearing the sacred thread." Nandanar, thinking this lowly birth was an impediment, had fallen asleep exhausted. Therefore, the Lord, realizing that Nandanar's sorrows would not cease unless his thought was removed, graced him thus to remove it. "You plunge into the fire and come. And when you come after plunging into the fire, come to Me with the Brahmins wearing the sacred thread." That is why the Lord graced him thus. The Lord's intention here was to alleviate the sorrow of Nandanar, the devotee who was distressed and slept, unable to worship the Lord dancing in the sacred hall of Tillai. The phrases "ippiravi pōy nīṅga" (so that this birth may cease) and "eriyiṉ kaṇ im māyap poyttakaiyum oru vazhittu" (this illusory false body will also be removed in the fire) imply that Nandanar's previous body ceased to exist, and his body was renewed as a meritorious body. That is why, after "ippiravi pōy nīṅga," Nandanar rose from the fire in the form of a sage, with the sacred thread shining on his chest and matted locks on his head. Therefore, the Lord did not say this to confirm that there is highness, lowness, or inferiority in caste. The thought about his birth was in Nandanar's mind. Therefore, the Lord's intention was to change that thought. Therefore, it is very wrong to make new explanations that the Lord spoke of highness or lowness in castes. However, this should not be interpreted to mean that there are no distinctions in castes, lineages, and other categories. Caste, lineage, and other distinctions are inherent realities. Saiva Siddhanta states that the fruits of our prarabdha karma (destiny) come to us through three things: caste, lifespan, and experience (bhoga). These are prescribed by the Agamas and other scriptures given by the Lord, and by the rules of Tamil grammar. It is our scripture that people, by following these prescribed rules, will gradually attain a higher state that transcends these rules. Therefore, those who make new researches, saying that the Lord smiled with a hint of disdain, seeing Nandanar's state of mind, who performed service according to the rules of the traditions He Himself established, are making erroneous researches, not correct ones. The Lord's gentle smile (punmuruvāl) is the natural smile that arises to bestow grace. "Nāyakaṉ tiruvuḷḷattu magizhndē aṅgaṇ eytiya muṟuvalum tōṇṭa" (A smile appeared on the Lord's joyful sacred face) is from Tirukkuṟippu Toṇḍar Puranam. "Tirumuga muṟuval seyya oṉṟiya viḷaiyāṭṭōrār" (They did not know the unified play when a smile appeared on His sacred face) and "aruḷ pozhiyum tirumugattil aṇi muṟuval" (a beautiful smile on the grace-showering sacred face) are some of the places where the Lord's gentle smiles are mentioned. Appar Swamigal sings, "Siritta mugam kaṇḍa kaṇ koṇḍu maṟṟu iṉi kāṇpatu eṉṉē" (Having seen the smiling face, what else is there to see?) desiring to have the bliss of seeing this sweet gentle smile of the Lord. Appar Swamigal sings that after seeing the Lord's gentle smile, what else can one see besides that? Let us continue our contemplation. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 08 — TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 207, Mummaiyāl Ulagāṇḍa Sarukkam, TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar Puranam. The Lord says, "This birth, which you consider lowly, and which you believe is an impediment to worshipping the dance in the sacred hall, shall cease. Plunge into the fire and come forth." We should recall here that Appar Swamigal, to purify his body after being in the Jain religion, prayed to the Lord to brand him with the trident and bull, saying, "Puṉṉeṟiyām amaṇ samayattoḍakkuṇḍu pōṇda uḍal taṉṉuḍaṉ uyir vāzha taraiyē nāṉ taripatataṉukku eṉṉuḍaiya nāyaka niṉ ilacsiṉai iṭṭu aruḷ" (This body, entangled in the evil Jain religion, let me live on earth with it, my Lord, grace me by branding me with your mark). Therefore, to remove the sorrow that Nandanar felt, the Lord went into Nandanar's dream and told him, "Plunge into the fire and come forth." The Lord similarly went into the dreams of the three thousand Tillai Brahmins individually and commanded them to prepare the fire. The ascetic Brahmins of Tillai, hearing the command of their Lord, all gathered before the sacred gate of Ambalavanar with fear. They gathered before the Perambalam, adjacent to the Anukkan Tiruvayil (inner sacred gate) in the great Tillai temple. Everyone gathered there with fear. This is because they feared that they had not been able to recognize such a great person, who was blessed by the Lord's grace, until now, and that they had neglected him. Furthermore, they also feared what the other citizens and the king would think or say if they kindled a fire as commanded by the Lord and made someone plunge into it and emerge. This is because the command to kindle the fire and have Nandanar enter it was conveyed by the Lord to the Tillai Brahmins in their dreams. But they feared what the citizens, the king, and others, who did not know the greatness of this being the Lord's command and Nandanar's greatness, would say. Later, in the same Tillai, Umapati Sivachariar, having received a written command from Nataraja Peruman, initiated and granted liberation to Pettan Samban. But the king and people of that country, who did not know this, came to Umapati Sivachariar and questioned him. Then he similarly granted liberation to a thorny bush and sent it to the world of liberation. Therefore, only the Tillai Brahmins and Nandanar knew that the fire was to be kindled and Nandanar was to enter it by the grace of the Lord. But they feared what the other people and kings, who did not know this, would think. Then, love overcame all that fear. Therefore, with great love for Nandanar, all the Tillai Brahmins came to Nandanar, the devotee. The three thousand Tillai Brahmins came to Nandanar and spoke as follows. Sekkizhar Peruman composed the following verse: "Ayyarē Ambalavar aruḷāl ippozhutu aṇaiṇdōm veyya azhal amaittu umakku tara vēṇḍi eṉa viḷamba naiyum maṉa tiruttoṇḍar nāṉ uyṇdēṉ eṉa tozhutār. Deyva maṟai muṇivarkaḷum tī amaittapaḍi mozhindār." This is the verse composed by Sekkizhar Peruman. Its meaning is as follows: The Tillai Brahmins addressed Nandanar, saying, "Ayyarē, by the grace of Ambalavar, we have come now, desiring to prepare a fierce fire for you." The devotee, Nandanar, whose mind was melting, bowed and said, "I am saved." The divine Vedic sages also prepared the fire as they had been told. This is the explanation of the verse. Now, the commentary on this verse. The three thousand Tillai Brahmins came and addressed Nandanar as "Ayyarē." "Ayyarē" means "O great one." Therefore, "Ayyar" is a term used to mean a great person. It is a recent custom for this to be used as a caste name. In ancient times, "Ayyar" was used to mean a great person. It is a later custom for it to be used as a caste name, not just for greatness, but as a conventional term. We can also recall here that Aludai Pillaiyar (Tirugnanasambandar) addressed Tirunilakanta Yazhppanar as "Ayyar nīr uḷam magizha iṅgaṇainda uruti uḍaiyōm" (O Ayyar, we are firm in our resolve to come here to make your heart rejoice) and "Ayyar nīr avatariṭṭiḍa ippatiṉ aḷaviṉ māṭavam muṉbu seyṭavāṟu" (O Ayyar, how much great penance this place performed before you were born) and "Ayyar nīr yāzh itaṉai muṟikkum" (O Ayyar, you will break this yazh). "Ai" is an exclamation of wonder, according to Tolkappiyam. "Ayyar" means one who is worthy of wonder. Therefore, Sivakavimaṇi Ayya writes that one should not introduce new researches into this story that are inconsistent with the conclusions of spiritual texts, thereby causing trouble and committing sacrilege. Therefore, the Tillai Brahmins also addressed Nandanar, the pure and great devotee, as "Ayyarē." Addressing him thus, they said, "Ayyarē, you are a great person. But we did not realize your greatness. Ambalavanar has graciously revealed it to us. Therefore, we have now had the opportunity to see you." As the saying goes, "Kāṭṭuvittāl ār oruvar kāṇātārē kāṇpār ār kaṇṇudalāy kāṭṭākkālē" (Who would not see if shown? Who would see, O Lord with the eye on your forehead, if not shown?). They said this with the implication that they have now come, realizing it because the Lord revealed it. If not for the Lord's grace, they would not have been able to come even now. They said they have come to kindle the fire as the Lord desired. That fire is the fire desired by the Lord. Therefore, it is also the fire desired by you. Fire is something that others would consider heat and avoid with fear. But that fire is something that you desire to enter. Furthermore, it is our tradition to refer to birth as heat. The fire that helps to alleviate the heat of such a birth is also fire. Just as poison is countered by poison, and the dirt of cloth is removed by another dirt, which is alkaline earth, it seems the Lord intended to remove the dirt of low birth from Nandanar's heart with fire. Therefore, the Tillai Brahmins said that they have come to prepare the fire as per the Lord's command. The Lord could have directly informed Nandanar alone to prepare the fire and enter it. But the Lord informed the Tillai Brahmins and had them prepare the fire. Why? Because Nandanar had the consciousness of his birth. To make Nandanar realize that he would come with a meritorious body, having shed his previous body, between the sacrificial halls, Vedic schools, and three thousand offerings, for Nandanar, who grieved how he could worship the Lord dancing in the hall, and to enable the devotees and the rest of the world to witness the power of grace and the power of his love, the Lord commanded the Tillai Brahmins to prepare the fire. Thus, Nandanar, hearing the announcement from the Tillai Brahmins, felt his mind melt with the emotion that he, a very humble and simple person, had received a great blessing. "Ittaṉaiyum emparo Ayya Ayya Emperumān tirukkaruṇai iruṇdavārē" (All this, O Lord, O Lord, how great is the grace of our Lord!) – thinking thus, his mind melted and softened. Nandanar says, "I am saved," with the conviction that he has received the great blessing of shedding this lowly birth, attaining a good body, and worshipping the Lord dancing in the hall, surrounded by the Tillai Brahmins. Furthermore, he says, "I am saved," having seen the Brahmins come in reality to fulfill the Lord's grace that he had seen in his dream. Thus, having received Nandanar's consent, the Tillai Brahmins prepare the fire as commanded by the Lord. They then inform Nandanar of this. When one sees the Lord's gracious intention and a soul ready to receive that grace, the action of the Acharya is to perform the appropriate ritual and unite that soul with the Lord's feet. That action of the Acharya is taking place here through the Tillai Brahmins. Furthermore, we must remember that Nataraja Peruman is also one of the Tillai Brahmins. Let us continue our contemplation. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 09 — TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 208, Mummaiyāl Ulagāṇḍa Sarukkam, TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar Puranam. As commanded by the Lord, the Tillai Brahmins, having obtained Nandanar's consent, prepared the fire outside the southern wall of Tillai, in front of the sacred gate. The place where the Tillai Brahmins prepared this fire is now a sacred pond called Omakulam. We can understand that this pond is the place where the Tillai Brahmins prepared the fire, and that in ancient times, the outer wall of Tillai and its fortifications, along with a sacred gate, existed on the southern side of Tillai, near this place. A Nandanar temple also exists near this Omakulam today. This temple was built and is celebrated by the lineage in which Nandanar was born. By the complete grace of the Lord, by the grace the Lord bestowed upon Nandanar, and by the grace the Lord bestowed upon the Tillai Brahmins, and by the Lord's grace, which always fills the countless souls who hear this story and wish to be saved, Nandanar enters the fire prepared by the Tillai Brahmins. Before entering, he circumambulates the fire, remembering the Lord's sacred feet, and then enters the fire, still remembering the Lord's sacred feet. What does this mean? It means that Nandanar, losing his self-awareness, surrendered himself completely to the Lord's grace and entered into that great act of complete surrender within the Lord's grace, thinking of nothing else but the Lord's sacred feet, in a state of lost self-consciousness, under the influence of the Lord's grace. Similarly, we saw in the Puranam of Amarniti Nayanar how Amarniti Nayanar, chanting the five-letter mantra, ascended the balance scale. Here, Nandanar was not one who had practiced the Lord's five-letter mantra. Therefore, as a symbol of its goal, the goal of the sacred Panchakshara, he circumambulated the fire, remembering the Lord's dancing sacred feet, and entered it. This fire was kindled by the Lord's command. Therefore, Nandanar thought that this fire was a Shiva sacrament that would remove his lowly birth and grant him a meritorious body to worship the Lord dancing in the hall. Therefore, he circumambulated it. Then he folded his hands and bowed with his body. He had already praised, "I am saved," and worshipped with his words. Thus, he entered the fire. Sekkizhar Peruman composes the verse: "Kai tozhudu naṭamāḍum kazhal uṉṉi azhal pukkār eytiya appozhutiṉ kaṇ eriyiṉ kaṇ im māyap poyttakaiyum uru ozhittu puṇṇiya māmuṉi vaḍivāy mey tigazh veṇṇūl viḷaṅga vēṇi muḍi koṇḍu ezhundār." (Nandanar, folding his hands, remembering the dancing feet, entered the fire. At that very moment, in the fire, he cast off this illusory false form and rose in the form of a meritorious great sage, with the sacred white thread shining on his chest and matted locks on his head.) Nandanar, folding his hands, remembering the sacred feet of the Lord who performs the five divine functions, entered the fire. At that very moment of entering, in the fire, he cast off this illusory false form, which is a product of maya, and rose in the form of a meritorious sage, with the sacred white thread shining on his chest and matted locks on his head. This body is an illusory false body, a product of maya. What is that falsehood? It is the body that is prone to pride in caste, lineage, and birth, and is also impermanent. Therefore, it is an illusory false body. Nandanar cast off that body, which he considered a lowly birth, a pulai form. Nandanar, having cast off that false body in the fire, rose from the fire, having obtained a true body, the form of a meritorious sage. How did he rise? With the three-stranded white sacred thread shining on his chest and matted locks on his head, which are the excellent symbols of Saivism, he rose from the fire. Sekkizhar Peruman composes the next verse: "Seṇtī mēl ezhum pozhutu seṇmalar mēl vaṇd ezhuṇda aṇdaṇaṉ pōl tōṇḍiṇār. Aṇdara tuṇdupi nādam vaṇd ezhuṇdatu uyar visumbil. Vāṉavarkaḷ magizhndu ārtu paiṇduṇar maṇdārattiṉ paṇimalar māṟigaḷ pozhiṇdār." (As he rose from the red fire, he appeared like the Brahmin who rose from the red lotus. The sound of celestial drums arose in the high sky. The celestials rejoiced and showered fragrant Mandara flowers.) Thus, Nandanar, having entered the red fire and risen as a meritorious sage, appeared like Brahma, seated on a red lotus flower. At that time, the sound of celestial drums arose in the vast sky. The celestials rejoiced and showered Mandara flowers. The red fire is compared to a red lotus, and Nandanar, who rose as a meritorious sage from that fire, is compared to Brahma, the Brahmin seated on a red lotus. Brahma is said to have emerged from the red lotus that arose from Vishnu's navel during creation. This is the scriptural tradition that Brahma emerges from Vishnu's navel during creation. Sekkizhar Peruman uses this simile here to explain that Nandanar, having cast off the illusory false form of low birth, appears in the form of a sage by the grace of Lord Shiva. This simile is used to illustrate that Nandanar shone like Brahma, who comes forth to create the world during creation. Furthermore, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that when he rose from the fire, he appeared like Brahma. He does not use this simile when he goes to worship the Lord dancing in the hall. This implies that even Brahma is not equal to him. The Tillai Brahmins, who were blessed, seeing Nandanar thus rise from the fire as a meritorious sage, folded their hands in worship. After the Lord had graced them in their dream, when they saw Nandanar outside the wall, a fear arose in the Tillai Brahmins, wondering what this was. The Brahmins stood bewildered, not knowing what to do. Now, seeing the wonder of the Lord's manifestation of grace, they folded their hands. All the devotees fell down and prostrated, immersed in great joy. The Vedic sage, TirunAlaippOvAr, comes to worship the sacred golden feet that perform the great dance of grace in the sacred hall, which is surrounded by the chanting of the Vedas in Tillai. He comes with eagerness, his inner desire to worship those sacred golden feet, which he had wished to worship for many days when he lived in Adhanur, now about to be fulfilled. Nandanar, who had previously said, "I will go tomorrow, I will go tomorrow," referring to a future event, now comes, knowing that that future event is about to be realized in the present. He comes accompanied by the three thousand Tillai Brahmins. Nandanar, TirunAlaippOvAr, goes directly, without any hindrance, not expecting anything else, now that the lowly birth, which he considered an impediment to worshipping the Lord dancing in the hall, has ceased. The Brahmins also followed him. For those coming from Adhanur, the first thing they see is the southern gopuram. Therefore, TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar crosses the southern wall and comes to the southern gopuram. He enters through the southern gopuram. It will be understood later that Tirugnanasambandar also came through the southern gopuram of this temple. TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar enters through the southern gopuram. Like the force with which water, long dammed and imprisoned, bursts forth when the embankment breaks, so too TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar entered, with the intensity of his desire, which had been suppressed by the thought of his lowly birth, now rising to its peak. After crossing the sacred gate and the gopuram, one should circumambulate the sacred street, and then worship the mansions and the Perambalam (great hall). The method of worship is to cross the inner sacred gate and then see the Nataraja in front of the Chidambaram (Chitrambalam). We must understand that our great Acharyas (spiritual teachers) have shown this method of darshan. Without that, TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar, after bowing to the gopuram and crossing it, quickly went to see the dancing limit. This indicates his intensity. For TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar, whose birth had ceased as the Lord had graced him, "ippiravi pōy nīṅga eriyiṉ iḍai nī mūzhgi muppuri nūl mārpuḍaṉ muṉ aṇaiyāy" (so that this birth may cease, you plunge into the fire and come before me with the sacred thread on your chest), only attaining the Lord remained. Therefore, TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar quickly went and merged. He reached the sacred feet of Chidambaram, which is the dancing limit, and merged completely into the pervasive grace, disappearing. All those who accompanied him saw him enter through the gopuram. They could not see where he went after entering. They became unable to see TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar, who had merged completely with the Lord. Thus, when TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar crossed the gopuram and entered, and then disappeared, the Brahmins were amazed. The great sages praised him. Ambalavanar, the endless blissful great dancer, graced TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar, the devotee who had come to Him, to remain worshipping His beautiful lotus-like feet, having removed the impurities of karma. Seeing the wonder of "aruḷum mey neṟi poy neṟi nīkkiya atisayam kaṇḍōmē" (we have seen the wonder that removed the false path and revealed the true path of grace) from Tiruvāsakam, those who saw TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar's form disappear, unable to be seen, experienced an indescribable sense of wonder. That is what is called a wonder. "Nalaippovar" (one who will go tomorrow) was a future event. "Varugiṇṟār" (he is coming) became a present event earlier. That too has now reached its complete fulfillment. Therefore, the poetic beauty with which Sekkizhar Peruman now sings "vaṇdaṇainda tiruttoṇḍar" (the devotee who has come and reached) is worthy of our praise. The Lord is a sentient being, and the soul is also a sentient being. Therefore, they can unite with each other, which is a principle of Saiva Siddhanta. "Aṇaindōr taṉmai" (the nature of those who have united) is a scriptural truth. The Brahmins were amazed. The great sages praised. Sekkizhar Peruman composed the verse: "Vaṇdaṇainda tiruttoṇḍar tammai viṉai māsu aṟuttu suṇdarattāmarai puraiyum tuṇai aḍikaḷ tozhutirukka aṇdamillā āṇanda peruṅkūttar aruḷ purindār." (The great blissful dancer, who is endless, graced the devotee who had come and reached Him, to remain worshipping His beautiful lotus-like feet, having removed the impurities of karma.) Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the Lord accepting Nandanar, TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar, as "vaṇdaṇainda tiruttoṇḍar tammai viṉai māsu aṟuttu." We should interpret "viṉai māsu aṟuttu" as "removing karma and impurity." The karma mentioned here is the two karmas, which is the karma mala (impurity of action). Here, it refers to the mūla karma (root karma). In "viṉai māsu aṟuttu," "viṉai" is the mūla karma. "Māsu" is the āṇava mala (impurity of ego). "Viṉai aṟuttu" should be understood as removing the mūla karma. "Māsu aṟuttu" should be understood as overpowering the āṇava mala. Āṇavam, kaṇmam, māyai are the three impurities. We can see that the māyā mala (impurity of illusion) was removed in the verse "im māyap poyttakaiyum oru ozhittu" (this illusory false body was also removed), where Nandanar, TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar, shed his old body and rose in the form of a meritorious great sage. Here, it is stated how the other two impurities were destroyed and removed. Thus, because all three impurities were removed and the removal of bondage (pāsa nīkkam) occurred, the great dancer, the Lord, graced TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar with His beautiful lotus-like feet. He is the endless blissful great dancer. As a concluding remark to this story, Sivakavimaṇi Ayya writes as follows: Many people are doing new researches into this story of Nandanar. Many new thoughts and movements regarding caste, lineage, tradition, and customs are arising in these days, causing conflict. We should not introduce these into this story and cause trouble. We should not confuse it. We cannot deny that among human beings, there are always high and low statuses based on birth and distinction, according to time and place. This is also ordained by divine texts. It is also known from human experience. Due to differences in karma and maturity, people and other beings are indeed of various kinds. This difference seen among people cannot be changed. These differences are included in caste, lifespan, and experience (bhoga). It is a truth discovered by spiritual texts that these prarabdha karmas come to us as ordained by the Lord, according to the karma of each soul. But it is also defined where this caste should stand. It is our duty to follow the rules prescribed in the dharma texts until one reaches the state where one has no actions of their own and stands as Shiva. When the state comes where self-awareness, also called soul-consciousness, falls away like a possession from a sleeping person's hand, then these states of caste and others also fall away and cease to exist. For Kannappa Nayanar, self-awareness ceased as soon as he saw the Lord's feet. After that, he transcended the state of caste-based conduct. Here, Nandanar's self-awareness was in his memory. The state of removing it would come only after removing that body. Therefore, to destroy his self-awareness, which is soul-consciousness, the Lord destroyed the body he possessed and graced him to rise from the fire, granting him a meritorious body. Caste, lineage, and other distinctions should be understood and followed only to the extent necessary. We should not allow them to become a source of pride, arrogance, and ego. This is emphasized by the Tiruvāsakam, "Sāti, kulam, piṟappu eṉṉum suzhippaṭṭu taḍumāṟum ātamili nāyēṉai" (me, a dog without self-control, who struggles caught in the whirlpool of caste, lineage, and birth). As a result of new researches, some new people have come forward, claiming that Sekkizhar Swamigal did not compose the part where TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar plunged into the fire and rose, and that these are later interpolations, and they try to find a place for their own theories by rejecting these parts. Many people from various castes became Nayanars and were praised by Sundaramurti Swamigal. Therefore, some conclude that there is no caste discrimination among people. Others claim that the Lord commanded Nandanar to plunge into the fire to show the true nature of the Tillai Brahmins, whom they consider to be hard-hearted people who, filled with caste arrogance, would not let anyone near them and kept them outside. These new researchers are conducting many new researches. TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar, from the time the thought of Tillai came to him in Adhanur, feared that it was not proper for him to enter Tillai, thinking of his caste, and remained outside. It was Nandanar himself who stood thus. No one prevented Nandanar from approaching the Lord. Similarly, no one told Kannappa Nayanar to come near the Lord and offer Him food. Furthermore, the Lord, who held Kannappa Nayanar and Tirukkuṟippu Toṇḍar by hand even without their sacred threads, brought our Nandanar near Him, making him a Vedic sage with a sacred thread and matted locks. This is because Nandanar's state of mind and conduct, who feared and lived by the thought that only if he were a Vedic sage or a Brahmin could he go near the Lord, was the reason for the Lord's grace.

Therefore, the Lord, as Nandanar wished, made him a Vedic sage with a sacred thread on his chest and graced him to come to Him. Therefore, we must identify all those who conduct new researches in this story that are inconsistent with the conclusions of spiritual texts and reject them. We should not listen to their opinions and commit sacrilege, writes Sivakavimaṇi Ayya. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman, stating that TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar, who plunged into the fire to shed his impure body and rose as a faultless Vedic sage, attained the sacred feet of Ambalavanar, and that he will now narrate the nature of the sacred service of Tirukkuṟippu Toṇḍar Nayanar with the help of the Lord's glorious sacred feet, concludes the Puranam of Nandanar, TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar, and begins the Puranam of Tirukkuṟippu Toṇḍar Nayanar. Let us also continue our contemplation. Praise to the sacred feet of TirunAlaippOvAr Nayanar. Praise to the sacred feet of Sundaramurti Swamigal. Praise to the sacred feet of Nambiāṇḍār Nambigal. Praise to the sacred feet of Sekkizhar Peruman. Praise to the sacred feet of Sivakavimaṇi Ayya. Thiruchitrambalam.
